# Preface

It is completely impossible to practice and follow true virtues if we do not establish <term>divine ethics<note>original: "göttliche Sittenlehre." This refers to a moral system grounded in religious revelation and God’s laws, rather than one based purely on human logic or social custom.</note></term> as our foundation!

However brilliantly the <term>natural philosophers<note>original: "natürlichen Weltweisen." Literally "world-wise ones," this refers to secular thinkers or philosophers who rely on reason and the study of the natural world rather than divine scripture.</note></term> have depicted the practice of virtue and the shamefulness of <term>vice<note>original: "Laster." A term for immoral habits or character flaws.</note></term>, and have therefore exhorted us to observe the former and avoid the latter; one can indeed admire the concepts they held of virtue and vice, and the teachings left behind in their writings. Their instruction, however, can by no means be considered sufficient to provide the strength required for the practice of virtue and the avoidance of vice; rather, it is—

<margin>Preface, Virtues, Ethics, Philosophers, Vices</margin>

<summary>In this preface, the author argues that secular philosophy is insufficient for true moral living. He asserts that while ancient philosophers described virtue well, only "divine ethics" provide the actual strength needed to live a virtuous life.</summary>
<keywords>preface, virtue, vice, divine ethics, philosophy, morality, natural philosophers</keywords>

# Preface

It is entirely impossible to practice and follow true virtues if we do not take divine <term>ethics<note>original: "Sittenlehre." This term refers to the systematic study of morals and conduct; here, the author specifies it must be "göttliche" (divine) to be effective.</note></term> as our foundation!

However brilliantly the natural <term>philosophers<note>original: "Weltweisen." Literally "world-wise ones," a common 18th-century term for secular thinkers or the classical philosophers of antiquity, such as Socrates or Seneca.</note></term> have depicted the practice of virtue and the shamefulness of vice, and have thus exhorted us to observe the former and avoid the latter; one may indeed admire the concepts of virtue and vice they held, as well as the teachings left behind in their writings, but their instruction can in no way be considered sufficient to provide the strength required to practice virtue and avoid vice. Rather, it is—

# Preface

—directed us toward a completely different <term>source of strength<note>original: "Kraftquelle." The author suggests that while human reason can describe what is good, it cannot provide the internal power needed to actually live a virtuous life; that power must come from a higher source.</note></term>, which can only be sought and certainly found in the revealed and fulfilled religion.

If the <term>natural philosophers<note>original: "natürlichen Weltweisen." Literally "world-wise ones," referring to thinkers like Socrates or Seneca who lacked access to Christian scripture.</note></term> recognized their moral teachings only through the <term>light of nature<note>original: "Lichte der Natur." A theological term for human reason and conscience as they exist independently of divine intervention or scripture.</note></term>, without divine revelation, and also endeavored with a sincere will to prove such teachings through a well-ordered way of life; then we would feel a pang of conscience to deny them a reward for their virtues after death. Indeed, we believe instead that such a <term>pagan<note>original: "Haide." This term refers to those outside of the Abrahamic traditions—specifically the "virtuous pagans" of antiquity who lived ethically without knowing the Bible.</note></term>, who lived virtuously only according to the light of nature without having divine revelation, deserves a greater reward than another person who was aided in their <term>way of life<note>original: "Tugendwandel." A person's moral conduct or the "path of virtue" they walk through life.</note></term> by the instruction and strengthening power of the revealed and fulfilled religion. # Preface

To the second, we count Jews and <term>Turks<note>In this historical context, "Turks" was commonly used as a general term for Muslims.</note></term>, who, alongside <term>natural religion<note>original: "natürlichen Religion." The belief that God's existence and moral laws can be understood through reason and the observation of nature.</note></term>, have also accepted <term>revealed religion<note>original: "geoffenbarte Religion." Knowledge of God and His laws provided through specific divine communication, such as the Torah or other scriptures.</note></term>. The natural, revealed, and <term>fulfilled religion<note>original: "vollbrachte Religion." A theological concept referring to the completion and perfection of divine law through the life and sacrifice of Jesus Christ.</note></term>, however, is a perfect religion, and is taught among the Christians.

According to this threefold distinction, the one God has also revealed His will in a threefold manner, and every generation will be rewarded or punished accordingly at the <term>end of days<note>original: "am Ende der Tage." A reference to the final judgment at the end of the world.</note></term>. 

The <term>Heathens<note>original: "Heyden." Referring to non-Christian, non-Jewish, and non-Muslim peoples who rely solely on the "natural" light of reason.</note></term> simply; the Jews and Turks doubly; the Christians, however, triply—depending on whether each has used or despised the <term>source of purification<note>original: "Reinigungsquelle." A metaphor for the spiritual means God provides to cleanse humanity from sin, such as baptism or the atonement.</note></term> assigned to them by God.

# P r e f a c e .

...who wants to live a morally virtuous life based on <term>natural religion<note>original: "natürlichen Religion." The belief that human reason and conscience are sufficient to understand God and morality without the need for scripture or divine revelation.</note></term>. If such a religion were sufficient to grant them the <term>blessed advantages<note>original: "glückseeligen Vortheile." The spiritual benefits and rewards expected in the afterlife.</note></term> they imagine for themselves, we would not dispute or refute their intentions and efforts. On the contrary, we would encourage them to walk this path, if we believed they could truly attain and advance their well-being through it.

However, love and duty compel us to warn all people—especially Christians—against this <term>misconception<note>original: "Vorurtheil." Literally "prejudice," but used here to mean a spiritual error or a false preconceived notion about the sufficiency of reason.</note></term>. At the same time, we wish to most faithfully show them a path through which they can attain true <term>blessedness<note>original: "Glückseeligkeit." A state of spiritual joy and divine favor, often contrasted with mere worldly happiness.</note></term> and peace of mind in this life, and secure it for eternity after death.

We are governed neither by spiritual excesses nor <term>fanatical ecstasies<note>original: "schwermerische Entzückungen." A critique of "Schwärmerei" (Enthusiasm), a term used at the time to describe radical religious groups who claimed direct, emotional, and often chaotic divine inspiration.</note></term>. Rather, we assert only those truths of which we, through a thirty-

No person will be deceived by our <term>tenets<note>original: "Lehrsätze." Referring to the afterlife or the timeless state of the soul after death.</note></term>.

# P r e f a c e .

...so that they can be read and usefully applied by all people.

It is all the same to us whether we are scolded or praised. Should we also be attacked, we say in advance: we will not engage in any <term>war of pens<note>original: "Federkrieg." A 18th-century term for a scholarly or literary dispute carried out through published pamphlets and letters.</note></term>, because we have learned our <term>sciences<note>original: "Wissenschaften." Here, this refers to spiritual, esoteric, or occult knowledge rather than the modern definition of physical science.</note></term> neither in lower nor in higher schools, but rather in the school of the <term>divine power of light<note>original: "göttlichen Lichtskraft." A mystical concept referring to direct spiritual illumination or the energetic power of the divine.</note></term>. Our sincere effort and intention are directed solely toward teaching and instructing all people, without distinction of religion, how they should conduct themselves toward God, toward themselves, and toward all their <term>fellow humans<note>original: "Nebenmenschen." Those who live alongside us; neighbors in the broadest sense of humanity.</note></term>, and how they can achieve and perform that—both within and outside themselves—which the <term>primordially one light<note>original: "unanfänglich einige Licht." A metaphysical description of the Divine as a singular source of light that has no beginning (primordial).</note></term> requires of them as its <term>creatures<note>original: "Geschöpfen." Created beings; the products of a divine creator.</note></term> for their own temporal and eternal well-being.

# Notes
## for unlearned readers. <note>original: "unstudirte Leser." This refers to laypeople or individuals who have not received a formal university education in theology or Latin.</note>

Anyone who wishes to examine our writings and learn to understand our meaning must first form a correct concept of the words found within them, as it would be difficult for those who have not studied to grasp our intent. We could indeed have exchanged these for others that are easier to understand, and made use of the words and expressions common in other theological writings, had we not found in the very composition <note>original: "Silbenfügung," literally "the joining of syllables." The author suggests that the specific structure or sound of these words carries a unique spiritual weight.</note> of the retained words something emphatic, evocative, stimulating to the powers of thought, encouraging, and reflective—which is the sole reason why we have kept them and made use of them.

**Magical:** <note>In the context of 18th-century mysticism, "magic" does not refer to sorcery, but to the internal, spiritual operations of divine grace or nature.</note> signifies, according to our meaning, mysterious powers incomprehensible to human understanding, whose effects can only be felt, but not seen.

**Moral:** modest, humble, lawful, as order requires.

<vocab>Magical, Moral, Composition of words, Powers of thought, Unlearned readers</vocab>

<summary>This page serves as an introductory glossary for the lay reader, explaining that specific mystical terminology has been chosen for its evocative power. It defines "Magical" as invisible spiritual forces and "Moral" as living in accordance with divine order.</summary>

<keywords>glossary, mystical terminology, magic, morality, lay readers, spiritual linguistics, 18th-century theology</keywords>

--- Page 27 ---
<meta>continuity with previous page: This page continues the glossary intended for "unlearned readers" that began on the previous leaf. It transitions from defining "Magical" and "Moral" to explaining more complex mystical concepts such as the nature of spiritual light, darkness, and the formation of ideas within the soul.</meta>

**Moral Light:** an understanding enlightened by moral conduct, which organizes one's life and actions according to divine and worldly laws; in general: virtuous.

**Moral Darkness:** signifies, by contrast, when the understanding is darkened, the will corrupted, the actions evil, and in conflict with divine and human laws or established order; in general: vice-ridden.

**Divine Light-Power:** By this is actually to be understood that incomprehensibly active power emanating from the light of the wisdom of the Father and the Son, which is called "Spirit" in the Scriptures, or as it is also understood and accepted in other theological writings under the name of the Holy Spirit.

**Idea:** These are images which the human understanding and earthly reason form within the human spirit through the mediation and help of the soul-fire <note>original: "Seelenfeuers." In 18th-century mystical thought, this "fire" often represents the fundamental driving force or vital spark of the human soul.</note>, and which are either extinguished again in the power of thought <note>original: "Denkungskraft."</note> by the human will, or else visibly represented in essence, or committed through actions.

<summary>This page continues the introductory glossary, defining "Moral Light" as virtue and "Moral Darkness" as vice. It also identifies "Divine Light-Power" with the Holy Spirit and describes "Ideas" as mental images fueled by the soul's inner fire.</summary>
<keywords>Moral Light, Moral Darkness, Divine Light-Power, Idea, Soul-fire, Virtue, Vice, Holy Spirit, Power of Thought</keywords>

# Chapter One.

## On the Origin of Creation through the Will of God, or the Supreme Good.

God as God is in Himself the most perfect being, and requires no creature nor created thing <note>original: "Kreatur noch Geschöpfe." In this mystical context, the author uses both terms to emphasize the totality of everything brought into existence, from sentient beings to inanimate matter.</note> outside of Himself. His understanding consists in the purest and most glorious light. He is the self-existent light, which no one can approach. He has within Himself no ground <term>ground</term> (German: *Grund*). In the mystical tradition, this often refers to the *Ungrund*—the fathomless, bottomless nature of the Divine that precedes all definition or creation. His depth and height, His power and strength, are known and understood only by His own Spirit. No one can see Him; nevertheless, it is permitted for those spirits and humans endowed with understanding and will to search into His incomprehensible depths, in which, however, a ground can never be found, because He has no ground. Even those with the <note>The text ends here mid-sentence; the catchword from the original German "höch-" indicates the next word is "highest" (höchsten).</note>

<summary>This opening chapter establishes the nature of God as a self-sufficient, perfect being of infinite light and depth. It introduces the mystical concept of God as "groundless," meaning His essence is an unfathomable mystery that nonetheless invites human contemplation.</summary>

<keywords>God, Creation, Divine Will, Supreme Good, Divine Light, Holy Spirit, Ungrund, Incomprehensibility, Perfection</keywords>

# Chapter One.

## On the Origin of Creation through the Will of God, or the Highest Good.

God, as God, is in Himself the most perfect Being, and requires no creature or creation outside of Himself. His understanding consists in the purest and most glorious light. He is the self-subsistent <note>original: "selbstständige." In this theological context, it refers to light that exists by its own power, independent of any external source.</note> light, which no one can approach. He has no ground <note>original: "Grund." This is a significant term in German mysticism (like that of Jacob Böhme), referring to the "Ungrund" or the bottomless, unconditioned nature of the Divine before any manifestation.</note> within Himself. His depth and height, His power and strength, are known and recognized only by His own Spirit. No one can see Him; yet, it is permitted for those spirits and humans endowed with understanding and will to search into His incomprehensible depths—though a ground can never be found therein, because He has no ground. Also those with the

<term>Creation</term>, <term>God</term>, <term>Will of God</term>, <term>Highest Good</term>, <term>Spirit</term>, <term>Understanding</term>, <term>Light</term>, <term>Creature</term>, <term>Created Beings</term>

<meta>catchword: high-</meta>

<summary>This opening chapter defines God as a self-sufficient, perfect being of pure light and "groundless" depth, whose nature is beyond full human comprehension but open to spiritual inquiry.</summary>
<keywords>Creation, Will of God, Divine Perfection, Sacred Light, Groundlessness, Holy Spirit, Mysticism</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the sentence from page 1, detailing the relationship between created spirits and the nature of God. It completes the thought that even the highest beings cannot fully grasp the Divine, shifting then to the purpose of creation itself.</meta>

highest understanding and the best will can neither reach His height nor fathom His depth, but can only recognize, honor, and worship Him in an eternal ascent. He possesses an indescribable wisdom. With one glance, He surveys the past, the present, and the future, and in this consists His joy, delight, and bliss <note>original: "Seligkeit." This term refers to a state of perfect, heavenly happiness or beatitude.</note>. He would not, therefore, have needed to create creatures outside of Himself, for they all stood within His knowledge and power of thought <note>original: "Denkungskraft." In this theological context, it suggests the divine capacity to hold all possibilities of existence within the mind before they are made physical.</note> as visibly present.

2. However, to also present these creatures visibly, to reveal and communicate Himself to them in love, and in turn to be recognized, loved, and honored by them as the highest good <note>original: "das höchste Gut." This refers to the philosophical concept of the *summum bonum*, the ultimate value from which all other goodness derives.</note>, and to be worshipped as their God and Creator—that was His ultimate purpose <term>Endzweck</term> <note>The final goal or "end" for which something is created; the reason for its existence.</note> for creation. His will was, therefore, the ground of the entire creation.

3. To our understanding, it is certainly incomprehensible how the will, without having any underlying matter <term>Materie</term> <note>The physical substance of the universe. The author is highlighting the mystery of *creatio ex nihilo*, or creation out of nothing.</note>, could make itself visible and be able to create and bring forth creatures.

<vocab>Understanding, Wisdom, Creation, Creatures, Ultimate Purpose, Matter</vocab>

<meta>catchword: The-</meta>

<summary>This page explains that God’s motivation for creation was not a necessity—as all things already existed in His mind—but a desire to share His love and be recognized by sentient beings. It highlights the mystery of how the divine will can manifest physical matter out of nothingness.</summary>
<keywords>Divine Wisdom, Purpose of Creation, Divine Love, Ground of Being, Matter, Summum Bonum, Beatitude</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 2 regarding the mystery of creation. The previous page ended with the thought that the divine will is the foundation of all existence, capable of bringing forth creatures out of nothing. This page cautions against trying to use human reason to grasp the mechanics of this divine act.</meta>

<page-num>3</page-num>
<sig>A 2</sig>

To investigate this is entirely in vain, and is not permitted to us, because as finite creatures <term>Kreaturen</term> <note>In this context, "creatures" refers to any created being, including humans, as distinguished from the Creator.</note> we can find no foundation, but would rather lose our reason, fall into confusion, and commit the same error into which our first parents <term>Stammeltern</term> <note>A traditional term for Adam and Eve.</note> fell when they wished to be like God. Outside of God, therefore, there was nothing. However, all creatures stood with Him eternally <note>original: "unanfänglich"—literally "without beginning," implying they existed as thoughts in the divine mind before time began.</note> within His idea, yet without essence <term>Wesen</term> <note>Meaning they existed as potential or ideas, but lacked independent physical or spiritual substance.</note>. But when His will spoke the word within itself: "Let it be!" <note>The divine command of creation, often referred to as the *Fiat*.</note> this will created an incomprehensible darkness <term>Finsterniß</term> outside of God.

4. In this darkness, the active properties formed themselves into Nature <term>Natur</term>. Among these active properties, a conflict <term>Streit</term> <note>In the mystical tradition of Jacob Böhme, which this text follows, "Streit" refers to the dynamic tension or interaction between opposing forces that allows the physical world to manifest.</note> arose. Through this conflict, fire—as a moving and creative force in nature—became manifest, and light was born from the fire.

5. These three properties—namely Darkness, Fire, and Light—are called the foundation of nature <term>Grund der Natur</term> by a highly enlightened theologian <note>This most likely refers to Jacob Böhme (1575–1624), whose "Theosophy" describes the birth of the universe through these specific stages.</note>.

6. Although these are indeed distinct from one another, and each has its own

<meta>catchword: We-</meta>

<summary>This page describes the transition from divine idea to physical reality, warning against the pride of seeking forbidden knowledge. It outlines a mystical cosmology where nature arises from the interaction of darkness, fire, and light.</summary>
<keywords>Adam and Eve, creation ex nihilo, divine will, darkness, Jacob Böhme, theosophy, fire and light, active properties of nature</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from page 3 concerning the active properties of Nature. The previous page concluded with the emergence of fire and light from a state of conflict within the darkness. This page explains how these forces function together and how they relate to the nature of God and the creation of humanity.</meta>

[...each] has active power <term>Wirkungskraft</term>, and each also conducts its own governance <term>Regiment</term> <note>In the mystical tradition of Jacob Böhme, "Regiment" refers to the way a specific quality or force directs and organizes its own sphere of influence.</note>; yet they arise from a single ground, conduct a communal governance in all creatures, and none can arise or exist without the others.

7. Just so has the essence <term>Wesen</term> of the one God revealed itself in three forces <term>dreyen Kräften</term>. And so He is called the one God in His threefold revelation <term>Offenbarung</term>.

## Chapter Two.
### On the Ground of Nature and its
### Mutually Conflicting and Opposing
### Properties.

---

Everything that exists outside of God has a ground <term>Grund</term> <note>In theosophical writing, "Grund" (ground or foundation) refers to the underlying basis or origin of a thing's existence, often contrasting with God as the "Ungrund" or the "Groundless One."</note> in and through the divine will. God alone has no ground, because He has no beginning and is a self-existent being.

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the four "watery properties" introduced on page 15, specifically how the astringent property reacts to the ignition of fire and how these properties manifest as vices within the human soul and as the "wrath of God" in nature.</meta>

-perty <note>This continues the word "property" from the previous page's final sentence regarding the "astringent property."</note> has, as it were, sunk back in terror and become the ground of nature, wherein through the incessant motion of the bitter and prickly <term>stachlichten</term> <note>In Böhme’s thought, this refers to the "sting" or "thorn" of restlessness that drives movement in matter.</note> properties, the fire is maintained and the progression of all creatures in the whole of nature is preserved.

4. In the soul of man, the astringent <term>herbe</term> <note>Literally "harsh" or "sour," this is the force of contraction and self-centeredness.</note> property is the root of avarice, because it is contracting. The bitter is the root of envy. The prickly is the root of pride, and the fiery is the root of lust, because the power of procreation lies within it, and it feels a burning desire for the mingling with the watery property.

5. Since these four properties work incessantly in visible nature as well as in the invisible soul of man, and also struggle incessantly within and against one another—with one property always wanting to rule over the others—they can cause terrible devastation in nature and in man if they are not subject to any law and governed by it; thus he <note>Referring to Jacob Böhme, the "enlightened theologian" mentioned on the previous page.</note> calls it the wrath of God in nature, or the properties of hell.

It transitions from describing these forces as potential sources of "devastation" to explaining their role as the necessary foundation for the human soul and the divine law intended to govern them.</meta>

6. These conflicting properties were thus capable of causing the greatest devastations in nature and creation <note>German: *Kreatur*; in this context, it refers to all created beings, both physical and spiritual.</note>, because God had placed great and powerful magical forces <term>magische Kräfte</term> <note>In Böhme’s philosophy, "magic" is not sorcery, but the spiritual power of the will that acts upon the "ground" of nature to create and manifest form.</note> within them; and for this reason He gave to the spirits created with understanding and will a law, according to which they should learn to overcome and govern the properties of this dark foundation <term>finstern Grundes</term> <note>The "dark ground" is the primal state of nature—the raw, unlit potential and restless desire that exists before being illuminated by God's light.</note> within themselves, so that they might not wander astray, but rather use them only as He would teach and instruct them through His light-power <term>Lichtskraft</term> within the conscience.

This darkness is thus the foundation of all nature, and there is no creature that does not possess the indicated properties within itself, whether in a physical or spiritual manner.

7. Just as these invisible magical forces are the fundamental effects in all creatures, so they are also the trunk and the basic roots of the soul in angels and humans; and the soul is nothing other than a magical fire that arises from this foundation.

8. The light emanating from this fire is its spirit, through which the fire, especially

9. This light is of an illuminating, beneficial, and refreshing nature; the fire, however, is of a burning, painful, consuming, spreading, and raging nature. 10. He can then determine whether these desires agree with the divine will or rebel against it, acting against the law impressed upon or revealed to the conscience, and whether they wish to live according to their own will.

## Chapter Three.

### On the Revelation of the Divine
### Powers through the Creation of
### Spirits or Angels.

When David says in the 4th verse of the 104th Psalm: You make your angels into winds, and your servants into flames of fire;

2. Thus he understands by the wind the swiftness in attending to and fulfilling the divine will; and by the fire, the great powers which He has imparted to them for the execution of the business <note>original: "Geschäfte" — here meaning the tasks or duties assigned by God</note> entrusted to them.

3. ## Chapter Four.

### On the possibility that morally good spirits can transform into morally evil spirits.

We would, of course, know little or nothing of this very important doctrine if the <term>Book of Revelation</term> <note>original: "Buch der Offenbarung"—while this can mean the specific biblical book, in this context it refers broadly to Holy Scripture as a divine disclosure of truths that human reason cannot find on its own.</note> had not come to our aid in this matter.

2. To human reason, this would be a mere fable, and every <term>rationalist</term> <note>original: "Vernunftsgeist"—literally a "spirit of reason," referring to individuals who rely solely on intellect and logic, a group often criticized by mystical writers for being spiritually blind.</note>, whether refined or coarse, would necessarily mock it; for it appears ridiculous that there should be morally evil spirits who, through their <term>magical power</term> <note>in this mystical tradition, "magical power" does not mean stage magic, but rather the innate power of the spiritual will to exert influence and cause changes in other beings.</note>, could also work and produce moral evils in other spirits.

3. Even we ourselves would have found it difficult to believe this with conviction and approval, had we not come to know—from the writings of a great theologian <note>This likely refers to the German mystic Jacob Böhme (1575–1624), whose "ground of nature" philosophy and descriptions of the spiritual "dark world" are central to this author's arguments.</note>—the properties of the <term>ground of nature</term>, including its evil effects and harmful consequences, after thirty years of investigation and examination...

<summary>The author explains that the idea of good spirits turning evil is a concept that defies human logic and can only be understood through divine revelation and the mystical study of the "ground of nature." He credits his own belief in these spiritual realities to three decades of studying a specific, influential theologian.</summary>

<keywords>spiritual transformation, revelation, rationalism, magical power, ground of nature, evil spirits, theology</keywords>

He established it within His human soul; and since our souls can now be **reborn** through Him—if we sincerely desire and will it—He is able and willing to essentially <note>original: "wesentlich." This suggests a substantial or "essence-to-essence" communication of divine power, rather than just a symbolic gesture.</note> communicate the power of His righteousness to us, so that we too [might possess] the righteousness within us

<summary>This page explains that while fallen angels lost divine righteousness completely, in humanity it was only "darkened," allowing for restoration through the "Second Adam" (Christ). # Chapter Five.

## Concerning the Beginning of the Creature.

In the **divine idea** <note>original: "göttliche Idee." In this context, it refers to the Neoplatonic or mystical concept that all things exist as eternal thoughts within God before they are physically created.</note>, no **creature** <note>original: "Kreatur." In historical theology, this refers to any created being, including humans, animals, and angels.</note> has a beginning; rather, everything is without beginning and eternal with and in Him.

2. In this privilege consists the highest perfection of His being, through which the power or the attribute of **independence** <term>Selbstständigkeit: Here used in the sense of "aseity," the theological concept that God exists in and of Himself, independent of any external cause.</term> is attributed to Him. He is, and is called, the Most High Being, because there is nothing above Him that might have **dominion** <note>original: "Herrschaft."</note> or power over Him.

<summary>This page warns that those who mock divine mysteries will share the fate of the fallen spirits and introduces the concept that all created beings exist eternally as ideas within God's mind.</summary>

<keywords>ground of the soul, divine idea, creation, independence, aseity, dominion, mystery, pride</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter Five, expanding on the nature of God’s will and the perfection of the original creation, specifically arguing against the philosophical idea that God had to choose from various options to create the "best" world.</meta>

3. He is the most perfect good and has placed His **fire- and light-power** <term>Lichtskraft: In the context of Christian theosophy (like that of Jacob Böhme), "fire" and "light" represent the foundational energies of God's manifestation—fire being the core of strength and light being the manifestation of love and clarity.</term> into the whole of creation, which is also sustained by the power emanating from Him.

4. He requires no deliberation and has no need for choice in His will, because His will desires only pure goodness, and the light of His understanding is so perfect that no darkness is found within it. John testifies to this in the 5th verse of the 1st chapter of his 1st Epistle, wherein he says: That God is a light, and no darkness is in Him. <note>Referring to 1 John 1:5: "This is the message we have heard from him and declare to you: God is light; in him there is no darkness at all."</note>

5. We would therefore commit a very great error and render Him capable of imperfection if we were to fall into this great delusion and suggest that when He created nature, and from it the world, He chose the best out of all possible worlds. <note>This is a direct critique of the "Optimism" theory proposed by philosopher Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, who argued that God chose to create the "best of all possible worlds" from a variety of mental options.</note> God is not capable of this imperfection; rather, everything He created was glorious, beautiful, and perfect. According to the attributes of the spirits placed within it—who were endowed with high understanding and a powerful will—it was a spiritual, **transfigured world** <term>verklärte Welt: A world transformed into a state of divine glory, light, and spiritual perfection, free from the density or decay of the current material world.</term>; how else could it

<summary>This page describes God as a being of perfect light and will who sustains creation through His own power. It explicitly rejects the idea that God chose the "best" of many possible worlds, asserting instead that His original creation was inherently perfect and transfigured.</summary>
<keywords>divine will, light-power, 1 John 1:5, perfection of creation, Leibniz critique, transfigured world, spirits</keywords>

## Chapter Six

### Regarding the Restoration of the First World

Thus the form of the first world came to an end, and thereafter this, our current world, took its beginning, of which Moses says in his narrative in the first and second verses of the first chapter of his first book <note>The Book of Genesis.</note>:

--- Page 116 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the transition from the "chaos" of Lucifer's fall to the biblical account of creation. It interprets the first verses of Genesis through a mixture of scripture and natural philosophy, specifically looking for alchemical principles in the primordial elements.</meta>

> In the beginning God created heaven and earth. And the earth was waste and void, and it was dark upon the deep; and the Spirit of God hovered over the water.

2. Through these words he <note>Moses, whom the author regards as the narrator of Genesis.</note> intends to say: that water, fire, and salt were together in a **mixture** <term>Gemische: This refers to the state of "chaos" mentioned on the previous page, where elements are confused and unrefined.</term>, and the Spirit of God hovered over this mixture.

3. After the true **philosophers** <term>Weltweisen: Literally "world-wise ones." In this context, it refers to sages or alchemists who seek to understand the hidden spiritual laws within physical nature.</term> investigated the active components of nature, they found that they lie hidden in water, fire, and salt or earth. Fire is the creative and active force; salt or earth is the **casing** <note>original: "Gehäuse"—meaning a shell or protective container.</note> in which the two forces of fire and light can be viewed, much like the soul within the human body.

4. Above this mixture now hovered the **spirit of nature**, which is likewise endowed with understanding and will by the divine **light-power**; so that it must work throughout all of nature according to the law placed within it by the Creator, so that it can bring forth all creatures according to their proper measure, at the right time, and in an established order.

6. Because it was a power created by God, however, and very great magical powers lay within it, Moses therefore called it the Spirit of God.

7. <meta>This page continues the interpretation of Genesis 2:7, moving from the literal text to an alchemical and esoteric explanation of the "clod of earth" used to create Adam. 14. One should certainly not fall into the thought that this clod of earth, from which Adam’s body was formed, was the same kind of clod of earth as the ground upon which we now dwell. Moses makes use of nothing but coarse images in his accounts, but within them the mystery lies hidden like a kernel in a hard shell. <note>The author is warning the reader against a literal "Sunday school" interpretation of the Bible, suggesting that the physical world we see now is "coarse" compared to the original creation.</note>

15. It would have been impossible for Moses to mean this literally, since he was a deeply experienced and well-grounded man in hermetic philosophy <term>Hermetic philosophy (hermetische Weltweisheit): A tradition of knowledge tracing back to late antiquity, involving alchemy, astrology, and the study of how the divine is reflected in the natural world. In this period, Moses was often regarded as a master alchemist.</term>, and thus knew well what lay hidden beneath the clod of earth, the air, and the fire. He only employed such images to stimulate and encourage his readers toward further reflection.

16. The inner foundation of the earth is Salt, Fire, and Water. <note>These are the three "primes" or principles often cited in Paracelsian alchemy (though usually listed as Salt, Sulfur, and Mercury; here the author uses Salt, Fire, and Water) as the building blocks of all matter.</note> Now, when these are stripped of all coarse parts, put together again, and have become a stone, it is a red, highly-refined earth. And the name Adam itself proves that it was such a clod of earth from which Adam's body was formed, because in the original language <note>original: *Grundsprache*. Referring to Hebrew, where the name "Adam" is linguistically linked to *Adama* (ground/earth) and *Adom* (red).</note> it signifies a "red earth."

...should give birth. If this proposition and conclusion were not correct, why then would God have united both reproductive powers within Adam’s body? He could just as easily have manifested each reproductive power in a separate form, as He allowed to happen with the animals through the **Spirit of Nature**. <note>The "Spirit of Nature" (Geist der Natur) refers to the governing force of the physical, material world, which manages biological life and animal instincts as opposed to divine, spiritual life.</note>

20. Adam’s body may have been somewhat denser than the body of an angel. # Chapter Seven.
## On the Separation of the Male and Female Procreative Power.

Thus Adam, through the conflict that arose within him over the divine commandment and his desire for the enjoyment of the beautiful fruit, was so weakened that he forgot spiritual procreation <note>original: "geistige Fortpflanzung." The author suggests that humanity was originally intended to reproduce through a spiritual or mental act rather than a biological one.</note>, and his beautiful body sank into a faint. This state is what Moses indicates by the expression *deep sleep* <note>A reference to Genesis 2:21. The author interprets this sleep not as natural rest, but as a loss of spiritual vitality.</note>, because in sleep we lie as if completely powerless, and yet our soul can still work upon images in the *Idea* <term>Idea: original: "Idee." In the context of 17th and 18th-century Christian mysticism, the "Idea" or "Sophia" is the spiritual mirror or blueprint of divine wisdom in which the soul can perceive truths.</term>. These images appear to our senses so clearly and brightly that we believe we are seeing them in their true essence and truly performing them.

2. His external senses had thus fallen into a *deep sleep*, and could not be stimulated by any visible objects. Now, the fire and light <term>fire and light: original: "Feuer und Licht." These are common mystical symbols for the soul's energy (fire) and its divine consciousness or love (light).</term> that remained unharmed in his soul had time and oppor-

<meta>catchword: tunity=</meta>

<summary>This page provides a mystical interpretation of Adam's "deep sleep" as a spiritual fainting spell caused by his inner conflict between divine law and physical desire. It explains that while his physical senses were dormant, his soul remained active in a realm of spiritual "ideas."</summary>

36. Fire pushes away from itself and also draws back toward itself. It creates air and pushes it away; yet, since it cannot live without it, it draws it back again. ## Chapter Eleven.

### Of the Hidden Meaning of Divine Revelation and Economy.

<note>The term "Haushaltung" (economy/dispensation) refers to the way God manages or administers the history of salvation for mankind.</note>

34. Now, in nature and the creature <note>The "creature" (German: *Kreatur*) refers to any created being, particularly humans, as distinct from the Creator.</note> there lies a twofold will, namely a <term>will of wrath</term> and a <term>will of love</term>. The will of love was intended to overcome the will of wrath and bring it under the divine will; for this reason, it <note>The will of love.</note> was without law. The will of wrath, however, stood under the law because it was meant to be obedient—as occurred with those spirits confirmed in goodness and with the Redeemer, and as still happens and must happen in all those who wish to partake in the love of God, the love of the Redeemer, and the heavenly attributes. # A Great Image of Animal Reason and Human Foolishness!

49. The rich man also remembered his companions, with whom he had feasted <note>German: *geschwelget*; to live in luxury, often implying overindulgence or carousing</note> while on earth. They would mock it within their own reason and further strengthen their evil intent, continuing their life of vice with an even stiffer resolve. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the Rich Man’s fate, moving from the description of his earthly lifestyle to a theological explanation of the nature of hell and the soul.</meta>

51. Since hell does not exist in any physical, observable location, but rather exists solely within a person through the harsh properties of the dark ground of the soul <note>original: *Seelengrund*; a mystical term referring to the deepest, innermost essence of the human soul</note>—from which the soul-fire burns, yet the divine light does not return into this fire, but instead separates itself eternally from the soul-spirit of the ungodly person at death—it is therefore an absolute impossibility for a new light or a new will to be ignited within the person to reunite with the divine light. Instead, the tree remains lying where it falls. Ecclesiastes <note>original: *Predig. Salomon*; The Preacher Solomon</note> chapter 11, verse 3.

52. If a person during their life has satisfied the harsh properties of the dark ground of the soul—whether in a coarse or a refined manner, aided by their cunning reason—against the voice of their conscience, then at death the divine light separates from the conscience. The conscience, together with the soul-spirit, then no longer has any foundation to which it can cling; thus, both are engulfed by the soul-fire and cast into the dungeon where eternal misery shall reign.

<summary>This page defines hell as an internal spiritual state rather than a physical place, explaining that at death, the divine light permanently departs from those who ignored their conscience, leaving them in eternal misery.</summary>
<keywords>hell, ground of the soul, soul-fire, divine light, conscience, soul-spirit, Ecclesiastes, spiritual death</keywords>

§3. The conscience is the worm <note>A reference to the biblical image in Mark 9:48 of the "worm that does not die," symbolizing the eternal pangs of regret and self-reproach.</note>, which now gnaws within the soul-spirit, and makes the most bitter and harshest reproaches to it: how it gave no ear to its admonitions and warnings, did not direct itself according to the divine will, but instead allowed the will to be governed only by those violent, sharp properties <note>original: *gewaltigen scharfen Eigenschaften*; in this mystical context, these refer to the raw, egoistic forces of the soul—such as anger or desire—that have not been softened by divine love.</note> lying within the soul. It must now attribute all guilt to itself for having fallen into this fire; and since this is of an eternal property, it must now remain therein eternally, without ever being redeemed therefrom again, nor being able to partake of even the slightest refreshment <note>original: *Labsaals*; comfort or solace.</note> either from the light of God or from the light of purified nature. Instead, it must live eternally in this torment and pain and die a perpetual, eternal death. This is the hell from which no redemption takes place.

55. Among the blessed, their nourishment is the divine, loving will of light, and among the unblessed <note>original: *Unseeligen*; those who are not in a state of grace or "blessedness."</note>, it is the dark, satanic will. From this law He does not deviate; even if the entire vast creation belonged to man as his own property, and he wished to give it to God to reconcile with Him, God still could not be turned away from His law. Rather, it remains according to that which was once spoken within Himself and revealed to mankind through the Prophet E- <margin>zekiel</margin>

[speak] will. 2. A thirty-year experience has confirmed us in this. These are not truths arrived at through mere brooding <note>original: "ausgegrübelten," suggesting intellectual striving or over-analysis</note>, but rather that Spirit has taught them to us who is the truth itself; and just as he has unlocked the meaning of the mysteries of the kingdom of God and his divine economy <term>divine economy: (German: Haushaltung) the management of the world and the unfolding of God's plan for salvation throughout history.</term> in our mind <term>mind: (German: Gemüthe) the seat of one's deepest emotions, will, and spiritual disposition.</term>, so they have also flowed through the pen onto the paper. 4. We impose these truths on no one; instead, we leave to everyone the freedom to accept or reject them. Let each person use them as their own inner light of understanding recognizes them; that is, they may praise them or they may criticize them, and we will not become proud because of the former, nor angry because of the latter.

5. Superstition and unbelief are the two dangerous reefs <note>original: "Klippen," meaning cliffs or rocky outcrops in the sea that cause shipwrecks.</note> upon which the greater part of the human race founders with its welfare—partly willfully, partly out of ignorance, and partly out of wickedness. We have therefore only wanted to show the harmfulness and danger of these two wrong paths, and at the same time point toward the goal which the Creator, out of love and compassion, has set for his human creatures who have deviated from him through false rational conclusions <term>rational conclusions: (German: Vernunftschlüsse) logical deductions or reasonings, here implying those divorced from spiritual truth.</term>. We do this so that they may recognize his will, accept it, submit themselves to it voluntarily, and thus participate in the goods of salvation <term>goods of salvation: (German: Heilsgüter) the spiritual blessings, graces, or benefits intended for humanity’s eternal well-being.</term> destined for them, through the enjoyment of his heavenly powers.

11. My reader! Your own will can make this path hard or easy for you; it simply depends on how you choose to position it. God does not force you to follow His will, but rather He has granted you a completely free choice in the matter.

But put it to the test: in which state do you find yourself better—when you have followed your own will or the divine will? After committing an evil deed, you have often endured great unrest, anxiety, and sadness in your soul <note>original: "Gemüthe," referring to the inner seat of emotions, will, and disposition.</note> through the reproaches of conscience, and you have wished that this deed or action had never occurred. If this vice attacks you once more, then oppose it with the divine will, and by its power, do not cease to struggle until you have weakened it within yourself and prevented its completion. Now examine whether your soul does not feel a peace and contentment for not having committed this evil act.

12. In this struggle, however, do not rely on your own powers of will, otherwise you are lost. The divine will alone—

A truly magnificent image of **spiritual watchfulness** <note>original: "geistlichen Wachsamkeit"; the practice of constant prayerful alertness against internal and external temptations</note>!

18. Now that you have tasted the power of the divine will, I know for certain that you will not deviate from it again, even if one offered to give you all the kingdoms of the world and their glory <note>A reference to the third temptation of Christ in the wilderness (Matthew 4:8)</note>.

In your **soul** <note>original: "Gemüthe"; a rich term in German Pietism referring to the seat of a person's thoughts, feelings, and moral disposition</note> you will sense such a peace, which with nothing ear- <meta>catchword: di= (earthly)</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the advice on judging temptations through the conscience and emphasizes the need for "spiritual watchfulness." It uses the biblical example of Nehemiah's wall-builders to illustrate that a believer must work and fight simultaneously.</summary>
<keywords>conscience, divine will, Nehemiah, Jerusalem, spiritual watchfulness, Gemüthe, spiritual warfare, temptation</keywords>

<summary>This page contrasts the spiritual fate of the worldly person, who is tormented by conscience and the "dark ground of nature," with the serene, charitable, and socially balanced life of the wise man living in the divine will.</summary>

--- Page 576 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the "wise man" from page 499. It completes the sentence describing his lack of affectation or social peculiarity, transitioning into his conduct within the family and his eventual transition to the afterlife.</meta>

forced <note>Completing the word from the previous page, "gezwungen," suggesting the wise man's behavior is natural rather than artificial or constrained.</note>. He eats, he drinks, he enjoys himself in a permissible manner with good friends. In his house, he rules as a dear father. Toward his wife, he is affectionate, and for his children, he is attentive and careful. He meets his **servants** <note>original: "Gesinde"; refers to domestic staff or help in a household</note> with love and composure. If they have done wrong, he corrects them again with an affectionate seriousness. He lets no opportunity for good pass by unused. His life is **virtuous**, his social interaction affectionate, his speech instructive, comforting, or admonishing. Thus he shines as a light, and walks as a child through the world. He looks forward to the end of his life with a confident courage, and heartily rejoices in the moment when his spirit is freed from the struggle of the **sharp properties of the soul** <note>original: "scharfen Seeleneigenschaften"; in theosophical mysticism, these are the harsh or conflicting spiritual forces within human nature that are resolved only through divine light</note>, and he can hand over and entrust the same into the hands of his God and **Savior** for the pure enjoyment of the heavenly properties, until he will be united again with his **glorified body**, and with body, soul, and spirit will attain the pure enjoyment of the **divine light**.

--- Page 577 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the character portrait of the wise man to a direct address to the reader. It synthesizes the previous lessons on the nature of God, specifically the dual manifestation of the divine will as both love and wrath, and places the responsibility of spiritual choice on the reader's free will.</meta>

20. My dear reader, here you have the true image of a real **wise man**. If you will now endeavor to diligently read our instruction, examine it closely, and investigate and judge it according to the Word of **Revelation** <note>original: "Offenbarung"; referring here to both the Holy Scriptures and the divine self-disclosure in the heart</note>, then you will learn to know God, yourself, and nature. You will come to see why the one God reveals himself in a threefold way, and how his single will could be transformed into a **will of wrath** and a **will of love** <note>In theosophical mysticism, this refers to the "dark" and "light" fires of God's nature—one consuming and the other life-giving</note>.
      In the one lies the spiritual eternal life, and in the other, the spiritual eternal death.

21. We have shown you wherein the **divine will**, his righteousness, heaven and hell, life and death, blessing and curse, love and wrath consist. You thus have two paths before you. It depends on your own **free will** which path you wish to choose; to the right or to the left. If you choose the path to the right, you are a child of God; if you choose the path to the left, you are a child of wrath or a devil. Upon the one

<summary>This page concludes the instruction on the wise man and challenges the reader to apply these lessons to their own life. It explains the dual nature of God's will (wrath and love) and emphasizes that every individual must use their free will to choose between the path of life and the path of death.</summary>

<keywords>wise man, revelation, divine will, wrath, love, spiritual life, spiritual death, free will, child of God, choice</keywords>

--- Page 578 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the two paths (left and right) introduced on the previous page. The text begins mid-sentence, completing the thought that once a path is chosen, the soul experiences the inevitable results of that choice according to divine law.</meta>

[path], he cannot deprive you of the enjoyment of heavenly qualities, and on the other hand, he cannot deliver you from hellish qualities, unless he were to destroy his own laws of **election** <term>Erwählung: The theological concept of God choosing individuals for salvation</term> or **rejection** <term>Verwerfung: The state of being cast out or denied divine grace</term>, and this he cannot do, because he is **immutable** <note>original: "unwandelbar"; meaning God's nature and laws do not change</note>. Only through your own **free will** can you confirm the divine will within you, or walk contrary to it; and thus you build for yourself your own heaven or your own hell within you. Your outward form does not change after death in the **resurrection**; only your will alone is either heavenly or devilish. Upon this rests your eternal welfare and your eternal woe. You are entirely free in your choice and not coerced; therefore, you must not attribute the blame to God, but to yourself alone, if you have chosen his **will of wrath** instead of his **will of love** <note>In this mystical tradition, God is viewed as having two "faces" or wills: a dark fire of justice/wrath and a light fire of mercy/love</note>. You are and remain in his power, whether it be in love or in wrath. If you walk in love, you will be comforted by his will; if you walk in wrath, you will be tormented by his will.

<summary>This page emphasizes that while God is unchangeable, humans possess the free will to determine their own spiritual fate. It argues that heaven and hell are internal states created by one's choices, and that God’s role as judge is a reflection of the path the individual has chosen to walk.</summary>

<keywords>free will, election, rejection, immutability of God, heaven and hell within, resurrection, will of wrath, will of love, divine justice</keywords>

--- Page 579 ---
<meta>This page continues the theological exploration of the human will, concluding that the choice between God’s "will of love" and "will of wrath" rests entirely with the individual. It then transitions into how this mystical understanding provides a "key" to interpreting the Bible, particularly the Book of Revelation, which the author feels is misunderstood by the intellectual elite of his time.</meta>

22. Within this dual will lies solely and uniquely the choice of your **election** <term>Erwählung: The theological concept of being chosen by God for salvation</term> or your **rejection** <term>Verwerfung: The state of being cast out or denied divine grace</term>. You are yourself the promoter of your eternal happiness or misfortune.

We have sought to unlock these great mysteries of His will and His **economy** <term>Haushaltung: Refers to God's "household management" or the divine plan by which He governs the universe and salvation history</term>, and to make them as comprehensible as the divine **light-power** <note>original: "Lichtskraft"; in mystical thought, this is the illuminating spiritual energy that allows the human mind to perceive divine truths</note> has permitted and allowed.

23. If one now recognizes and understands these two important forces acting within visible nature and within every creature, then one will also learn to judge with greater accuracy the good and evil events described in the Book of **Revelation**. In its simple, seemingly offensive, and sensory narratives, one will find such a profound meaning lying hidden that one will marvel at both its majesty and its simplicity. One will learn to value this book—which is so greatly despised in our own day—so highly that one would not trade it for all the libraries in the world, nor ever wish to be without it. Only to those "clever" ones of this world, who, according to their own will and **conceit** <term>Dünkel: Intellectual arrogance or the delusion of possessing wisdom through one's own ego rather than through divine grace</term> of wisdom...

<summary>The author concludes that individuals determine their own salvation or damnation through their will and asserts that understanding these divine "forces" is the key to appreciating the hidden depth of the Book of Revelation. He defends the scriptures against the intellectual skeptics of his era who view the biblical stories as overly simple or offensive.</summary>

<keywords>free will, election, rejection, divine economy, light-power, Book of Revelation, spiritual interpretation, conceit, mystical theology</keywords>

The natural man perceives nothing of the meaning of the Spirit of God; the divine will is **folly** <note>original: "Thorheit"; This is a direct allusion to 1 Corinthians 2:14: "The natural person does not accept the things of the Spirit of God, for they are folly to him..."</note> to him, and he cannot recognize it, for it must be

# List of Printing Errors.

---

In the Preface, page 13, in the 5th line, **Seelengrunges**, read **Seelengrundes** <term>Seelengrund: A central concept in German mysticism, referring to the "ground of the soul"—the deepest, innermost essence of a person where the divine and human meet.</term>.
Page 11. Line 14. **anbee** read **anbete** <note>original: "anbete"; meaning "worship."</note>.
— 29. — 24. **such** read **false**.
— 57. — 10. **detested** read **hated**.
— 59. — 13. **devout** read **suspicious**.
— 96. — 14. **images** read **images** <note>The correction fixes a grammatical case ending in the original German.</note>.
— 99. — 3. **now** read **only**.
— 99. — 12. **work** read **woman**.
— 108. — 10. **temptation** read **assurance**.
— 109. — 7. **that one** read **the only one**.
— 140. — 24. **hearing** read **brains**.
— 145. — 16. **outgoing** read **starving**.
— 163. — 13. **thereof** read **thereon**.
— 165. — 4. **life** read **of life**.
— 173. — 3. **bring** read **bend**.
— 178. — 11. **grace-beast** read **door of grace** <note>original: "Gnadenthier" to "Gnadenthüre." This is a significant correction; a "Gnadenthier" would be a nonsensical "beast of grace," whereas "Gnadenthüre" is the "door of grace," a common spiritual metaphor for Christ or the path to salvation.</note>.
— 181. — 23. **since it it though** read **since it though**.
— 216. — 11. **reached** <note>original: "verreichte"</note> read **attained** <note>original: "erreichte"</note>.
— 247. — 12. **mislead** read **disturb**.
— 247. — 15. **agenst** read **against**.
— 250. — 5. **the** read **the** <note>Correction of a grammatical article.</note>.
— 254. — 25. **of the spirits** read **of the Spirit**.
— 269. — 3. **in the** read **a**.
— 317. — 20. **holy** read **holy** <note>Grammatical adjustment to the adjective ending.</note>.
— 335. — 17. **actual** read **worldly**.
— 357. — 12. **the** read **the**.
— 537. — 14. **the** read **the** <note>This page number appears out of sequence in the original document, likely a secondary printing error in the errata list itself.</note>.
— 367. — 23. **willingly** read **fully**.
— 372. — 3. **now** read **only**.
— 378. — 5. **must** read **might** <note>original: "mußte" to "müßte"; changing the mood from indicative to subjunctive.</note>.
— 385. — 8. **therefore** read **nevertheless**.
— 386. — 13. **weakness** read **wickedness** <term>Schalkheit: This term denotes a sense of malice, cunning, or intentional wrongdoing, which the author distinguishes from simple "weakness" (Schwachheit).</term>.
